Last comment 2026-09-01 Re: [PATCH v2] btrfs: tree-checker: add qgroup status item check Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch adds a check for qgroup status item consistency in the Btrfs tree-checker, specifically looking for items that are missing the 'enable_gen' flag, which is new after the simple quotas change.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Qu Wenruo ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer questioned the effectiveness of checking the flags of a given item, citing potential false negatives if squota is not used or if the size is small, and suggested an alternative approach to identify qgroup status items missing enable_gen Reviewer Qu Wenruo pointed out that the tree-checker's principle is to verify every member of a variable-sized structure, and specifically noted that checking flags like RESCAN/INCONSISTENT may not be necessary for regular qgroups since it's valid for them to not have these flags set. However, he suggested adding a check in simple mode to ensure the RESCAN flag is not set.
Show original comment 
Yes, the reason is correct.

For those still running qgroups and have never disabled it (which I 
believe they are minority now), their qgroup status items will stay 
without the enable_gen member.

---

This is the common practice for tree-checker, remember that tree-checker 
is the "2nd best memory tester" to catch weird problems, not only from 
btrfs, but also bad hardware.

So the principle is not "this should not happen so we don't need to 
check" but "every member should be verified when possible".

And this is part of the common path for any variable sized structure, we 
need to make sure the added member is not beyond the item boundary.


Finally for the "why not look for normal ones missing rescan or flags or 
whatever" part, that's because for regular qgroups, it's completely 
valid to not have RESCAN/INCONSISTENT flag set, so we do not need to 
check that.

But this inspired me that, for simple mode we should not have RESCAN 
flag set.
I'll add this check into the next version.

Thanks for the review,
Qu

Last comment 2026-09-01 Re: [PATCH] btrfs: remove runtime tweakable feature sysfs interface Reviewed-by Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch removes the sysfs interface for runtime tweakable features in Btrfs, which allows certain behaviors to be dynamically adjusted without using ioctl or mount options. The removal is motivated by the fact that some features are not at whole filesystem granularity and the generic features mechanism no longer makes sense. This change simplifies the code and aligns with a desire to converge on using only ioctl for runtime feature setting.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Qu Wenruo ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Qu Wenruo expressed no issues with the current sysfs interfaces, finding them more straightforward and less prone to compatibility problems compared to mount options, but noted that there is a potential race condition with mount/unmount operations. Reviewer noted that the original assumption about features being enabled halfway was overly optimistic, and suggested that enabling work should be pushed into user-space programs rather than the kernel itself.
Show original comment 
Personally speaking I have no problem with the current sysfs interfaces 
at all.

It's more straightforward, less compatibility problems compared to mount 
options.

The only down side is the race with mount/unmount, but it's not a big 
deal as long as we're only modifying a single in-memory flag/value.

---

I think the features interface itself is totally fine, just we were a 
little too optimistic on the features that can be enabled halfway.

Nowadays if we want to introduce some new features, we will be more 
cautious and push most of the enabling work into progs other than the 
kernel itself, and may not choose to allowing mixed old and new structures.

Thanks for the review,
Qu

Last comment 2026-09-01 Re: [PATCH] rseq: defer time slice extension yield for sys_futex_wake Neutral LLM

Patch proposes deferring time slice extension yield for sys_futex_wake in rseq implementation.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Dmitry Ilvokhin Neutral LLM
Asked about publicly available implementation of a mutex combining rseq time-slice extensions and futexes, expressed interest in learning more about their interaction.
Alice Ryhl Neutral LLM
Provided pseudocode for a mutex implementation combining rseq time-slice extensions and futexes, discussed potential issues with preemption while holding the lock.
Show original comment 
On Tue, Sep 01, 2026 at 11:49:43AM +0000, Dmitry Ilvokhin wrote:
> On Mon, Aug 31, 2026 at 12:57:26PM +0000, Alice Ryhl wrote:
> > When a task is granted an rseq scheduler time slice extension, it is
> > expected to finish its critical section and relinquish the CPU via
> > rseq_slice_yield(2). If the task issues any other system call while a
> > grant is active, rseq_syscall_enter_work() forces an immediate
> > reschedule on syscall entry via cond_resched(). This may cause
> > significant latency penalty for userspace lock implementations that use
> > rseq time slice extensions when unlocking the futex.
> > 
> > In a userspace mutex unlock sequence:
> > 1. The lock is released in userspace.
> > 2. If there are waiters, the unlocking thread calls sys_futex_wake()
> >    to wake a sleeping waiter.
> 
> Just out of curiosity, is there any publicly available implementation of
> a mutex that combines rseq time-slice extensions and futexes?
> 
> I'm interested in learning more about how these two mechanisms interact
> with each other.

This came up while I was looking into implementing one for use in Tokio.
There, we have quite a few cases where we have a doubly linked list
protected by a mutex, and I really really want to avoid preemption while
that lock is held. Some of those locks are known problems for contention
in Tokio.

But that implementation is still WIP.

Some pseudocode:

struct rseq_futex {
    // 0 = unlocked, 1 = locked, 2 = contended
    int futex;
};

void mutex_lock(struct rseq_futex *mutex) {
    for (;;) {
        __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.request = 1;
        int expected = 0;
        // Change state from 0 -> 1 to lock.
        if (compare_exchange(&mutex->futex, &expected, 1))
            return;

        // lock is taken, use slow-path
        bool was_granted = __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.granted;
        __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.request = 0;

        // If the state is not already 2, then change it so that
        // mutex_unlock() knows to wake us up.
        if (expected == 2 || compare_exchange(&mutex->futex, &expected, 2)) {
            // State is 2, we can sleep until that changes.
            futex_wait(&mutex->futex, 2);
        } else if (was_granted) {
            rseq_slice_yield();
        }
    }
}

void mutex_unlock(struct rseq_futex *mutex) {
    // Unlock the mutex and return whether it was contended.
    int prev = atomic_swap(&mutex->futex, 0);

    // End the time slice extension for the critical region
    bool was_granted = __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.granted;
    __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.request = 0;

    if (prev == 2) {
        futex_wake(&mutex->futex);
    } else if (was_granted) {
        rseq_slice_yield();
    }
}

Though now that I think more about it, perhaps unlock should look like
this, to avoid a preemption point just immediately before futex_wake().

void mutex_unlock(struct rseq_futex *mutex) {
    // Unlock the mutex and return whether it was contended.
    int prev = atomic_exchange(&mutex->futex, 0);

    // Wake up contended waiters
    if (prev == 2)
        futex_wake(&mutex->futex);

    // End the time slice extension for the critical region
    bool was_granted = __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.granted;
    __rseq_abi.slice_ctrl.request = 0;

    if (was_granted)
        rseq_slice_yield();
}

[PATCH 0/5] KVM: guest_memfd: bind backing memory to a NUMA node 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm/mempolicy: add mempolicy_create()
  • [2/5] mm/mempolicy: add mpol_set_shared_policy_range()
  • [3/5] KVM: guest_memfd: bind backing memory to a NUMA node at creation
  • [4/5] selftests: KVM: guest_memfd: let the gmem_test() harness bind a node
  • [5/5] selftests: KVM: guest_memfd: test GUEST_MEMFD_FLAG_BIND_NODE
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series introduces a new flag, GUEST_MEMFD_FLAG_BIND_NODE, to the KVM guest memory file descriptor (memfd) API. When set, this flag binds the backing memory of the memfd to a specific NUMA node, ensuring that all allocated folios are placed on that node. This is done by creating an MPOL_BIND policy for the requested node and installing it over the whole inode. The patch series includes preparatory work in the mm/mempolicy code, as well as selftest harness support and testing. The approach taken is to bind each fd to a single host node, rather than allowing interleaving across multiple nodes, which would defeat guest NUMA balancing decisions.

This patch refactors the memoryless node check in mm/memory_hotplug.c by extracting it into a separate function, node_is_memoryless(). The existing code is simplified and made more readable. No functional changes are introduced.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-08-29 Last comment 2026-09-02 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] mm/mempolicy: stop copying state in the interleave paths 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm/mempolicy: use SRCU for the weighted interleave state Submitted 2026-08-29
  • [2/2] mm/mempolicy: stop copying the nodemask in the interleave paths Neutral Submitted 2026-08-29Last comment 2026-09-02 Rakie Kim Gregory Price (author)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch eliminates unnecessary copies of nodemask and node weights in the interleave paths of the Linux kernel's memory policy module. It achieves this by using SRCU to pin node weights in place, operating directly on pol->nodes for nodemasks, and clamping the walk to a single pass. The changes improve performance but may introduce a small trade-off in distribution accuracy during rebind operations.

Rakie Kim noted that the patch does not address the case where a node is removed from the system, which would cause the interleave node selectors to fall back to numa_node_id() and potentially return an incorrect result. Reviewer Rakie Kim agrees that using SRCU to resolve the sleeping-allocation problem is a good approach and suggests continuing in this direction. Reviewer noted that after the patch, the loop bound no longer matches the mask if it changes between reads, and suggested counting nodes in the weight sum loop like weighted_interleave_nid() does
Show original comment 
Hello Gregory,

Thanks for the series.

On Fri, 28 Aug 2026 21:59:43 -0400 Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net> wrote:

---

I went through both patches. Resolving the sleeping-allocation
problem with SRCU rather than patching the allocation site, and
removing the copies from the fault path along the way, looks like
the right direction to me.

---

I have a minor comment on this part. After this change, everything
else reads pol->nodes fresh at the point of use - the weight sum
and the walk both look at the current mask. Only nnodes is still
the count from this earlier read. If the mask changes in between,
the loop bound no longer matches the mask the loop is actually
walking, so the walk can stop short of the pages the weight total
planned for. Would it be better to count the nodes in the loop
that sums the weights, the way weighted_interleave_nid() does it
in this patch?

	nnodes = 0;
	for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes) {
		weight_total += table ? table[node] : 1;
		nnodes++;
	}

[...snip...]

Thanks again for your time.

Rakie Kim
View on lore ↗
Signals: patch does not address edge cases, agreement, approval
Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Rakie Kim Needs Work LLM
Author responded to Rakie Kim's feedback, stating that widening the SRCU window to include the cpuset cookie does not fix anything and may even be counterproductive by calculating weight totals unnecessarily.
Show original comment 
I don't think this actually fixes anything?

But basically the proposal is to widen the SRCU() window further to
include the cpuset cookie entirely.

e.g.

SRCU() {
	cpuset_cookie() {
		for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes)
			weight_total += ...
			nnodes++;
	}

	/* ... snip - single node quick-exit ... */

        /* ... actual multi-node bulk allocation ... */
	for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes) {
		nr_allocated = __alloc_pages_bulk(gfp, node, ...);

		/*
                 * At this point, due to a torn read from pol->nodes
		 * we can visit a node that wasn't present previously
		 * or we can skip a node that was present previously.
		 *
		 * In either case, weight_total is the wrong value for
		 * the set of nodes being walked anyway - we are going
		 * to skew in the distribution no matter what.
		 */
	}
}

I'm not sure widening the SRCU window is worth it here, it doesn't
actually buy us anything.

Also we'd be calculating the weight total every time even when there's a
scenario where we quick-exit because the entire allocation fits in the
first node in the mask.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-08-28 ⏱ No comments yet [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: take a cpuset cookie for the interleave node count Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a memory allocation issue in the Linux kernel's mm/mempolicy module, specifically in the alloc_pages_bulk_interleave function. The problem arises when a concurrent cpuset rebind can cause a torn read of the nodemask_t, leading to an empty mask and incorrect node count. To fix this, the patch takes a cpuset cookie around the count and retries if the mask becomes invalid.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Huang Ying Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Identified the issue as a torn read of the nodemask_t due to concurrent cpuset rebinds. Suggested taking a cpuset cookie around the count and retrying if the mask becomes invalid.
[PATCH 0/2] mm: refactor zonelist constructors and iterators 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm: refactor find_next_best_node to find_next_best_node_in Neutral Last comment 2026-09-02 Gregory Price Balbir Singh
  • [2/2] mm/page_alloc: refactor build_node_zonelist() out of build_zonelists()
Neutral LLM

The patch refactors the zonelist constructors and iterators in the Linux kernel to make them more flexible and explicit. Specifically, it introduces a new function `find_next_best_node_in()` that takes an explicitly defined candidate set, and extracts the per-node construction loop from `build_zonelists()` into a separate function `build_node_zonelist()`. These changes allow for building zonelists over custom candidate sets and iterating them in fallback order. The patch is a refactoring effort with no functional change intended.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Balbir Singh ↳ replying to Gregory Price Acked-by Positive LLM
Acknowledged the patch without any objections or suggestions.
Signals: acknowledgement

  • [2/2] sched/numa: scan read-only file mappings in tiering mode Neutral Last comment 2026-09-04 Gregory Price
Neutral LLM

The patch series aims to stop VMA scan filters from gating promotion in the Linux kernel's NUMA (Non-Uniform Memory Access) implementation. Specifically, it addresses two issues: the per-VMA PID filter and read-only file mapping exclusion. The patches modify existing code to allow for more efficient promotion of pages between memory tiers.

Last comment 2026-09-02 [PATCH v2 0/3] fs/nfsd: accept a backdated timestamp from a delegation holder 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] fs: stamp the current time for a stale delegated ctime update
  • [2/3] nfsd: accept a backdated timestamp from a delegation holder
  • [3/3] nfsd: compare CB_GETATTR times against the inode
Neutral LLM

This patch series addresses an issue where applications cannot set backdated timestamps on files after writing them, due to strict attribute delegation rules in NFS. The solution is to modify the server-side handling of ctime updates, allowing it to accept these backdated mtime updates. This is achieved by changing how the ctime is handled and comparing against the inode instead of grant-time snapshots.

[PATCH v3 0/3] fs/nfsd: accept a backdated timestamp from a delegation holder 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] fs: stamp the current time for a stale delegated ctime update
  • [2/3] nfsd: accept a backdated timestamp from a delegation holder
  • [3/3] nfsd: compare CB_GETATTR times against the inode
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch set allows the NFS server to accept backdated timestamps from a delegation holder, resolving an issue where applications that write files and then update their timestamps (e.g., tar -x, cp -p) cannot properly set these timestamps due to strict attribute delegation rules. The solution involves modifying how the change time is handled on the server side, rather than requiring changes on the client side. This approach aims to improve performance in common use cases while still enforcing necessary security and consistency requirements.

Neutral LLM

This patch series aims to harden the netlink listener set interface in the NFS daemon (nfsd) and SunRPC code by introducing early error handling, reducing unnecessary RPC calls, and improving timeout handling. The goal is to prevent hangs and improve overall performance.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Jeff Layton Neutral LLM
The reviewer suggested that the patches are a good start, but some of the changes may not be necessary. They asked for more information about the performance benefits and whether the changes can be made more incremental.
Show original comment 
nfsd_nl_listener_set_doit() matches each requested listener against the
existing set. The nested loop that does this is O(N * M), where N is the
requested count and M is the existing count. The loop runs under sv_lock
with bottom halves disabled. A userland request with a very large listener
list can therefore spin in atomic context for a long time.

Reject a request that carries more than NFSD_NL_LISTENER_MAX (1024)
entries. The check goes in nfsd_nl_validate_listeners(), before the code
takes any lock. The limit is far above any realistic configuration.

This patch does not cap M. Only the message size bounded N; real sockets
bound M. A listener_set result set is the requested set, so that path also
holds M at the cap, but __write_ports_addxprt() adds two listeners per call
and removes none, so repeated calls can push M past it. The worst case
under sv_lock is therefore 1024 * M, plus 1024 nla_parse_nested() calls.
Both interfaces require CAP_NET_ADMIN.

Fixes: 16a471177496 ("NFSD: add listener-{set,get} netlink command")
Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Jeff Layton <jlayton@kernel.org>

  * @info: netlink metadata and command arguments
  *
- * Walk every NFSD_A_SERVER_SOCK_ADDR attribute and confirm that each entry
- * is well-formed: it parses against the policy, carries both an address and
- * a transport name, and the address is long enough for its family. Doing
- * this up front lets the callers below assume every entry is valid and
- * guarantees we make no changes when the request is malformed.

+ * not oversized and that each entry is well-formed.

+			return -E2BIG;
+		}

Last comment 2026-09-01 [PATCH] nfsd: accept a backdated timestamp from a delegation holder Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch allows the NFS server to accept and honor backdated timestamps from a client holding an attribute delegation, rather than ignoring them or clamping them to the current time. This change is necessary because clients may intentionally set backdated timestamps during operations like cp -p, rsync -t, or tar -x. The patch updates the nfsd4proc.c file to accept backwards atime and mtime values from a delegation holder, while still enforcing rules for future times and maintaining consistency with RFC 9754.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Chuck Lever ↳ replying to Jeff Layton Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Chuck Lever questioned whether dp->dl_ctime is the correct value to compare against, noting that it's only sampled once when the delegation is granted and never refreshed; he also pointed out a potential issue where a client can send multiple SETATTRs with TIME_DELEG_MODIFY while holding the delegation, leading to inconsistent ctime updates.
Show original comment 
^^^^^^^^^^^^

Is dp->dl_ctime the right value to compare against here?  It is sampled
once, when the delegation is granted, and nothing refreshes it after
that:

fs/nfsd/nfs4state.c:nfs4_open_delegation() {
    ...
	dp->dl_atime = stat.atime;
	dp->dl_ctime = stat.ctime;
	dp->dl_mtime = stat.mtime;
    ...
}

Is there anything that stops a client from sending more than one SETATTR
with TIME_DELEG_MODIFY while it holds the delegation?

Say the first one carries an mtime T5 that is later than dl_ctime.
nfsd4_vet_deleg_time() returns true, ATTR_CTIME_SET stays set, and the
inode ctime becomes T5.

If a second SETATTR then backdates the mtime to T3, where dl_ctime < T3 <
T5, the comparison is still against the grant-time dl_ctime.  It returns
true again, so ATTR_CTIME_SET stays set and T3 is carried into ia_ctime.

notify_change() -> setattr_copy() -> inode_set_ctime_deleg() then drops
that update, because T3 is older than the T5 already in the inode:

fs/inode.c:inode_set_ctime_deleg() {
    ...
	/* If the update is older than the existing value, skip it. */
	if (timespec64_compare(&update, &cur_ts) <= 0)
		return cur_ts;
    ...
}

The mtime moves back to T3 and the ctime stays at T5, so the ctime is
neither carried from the mtime nor stamped with the current time.  That
looks different from the comment just above it, and from the commit
message: "An mtime that moves backwards sets the ctime to the current
time."

Would comparing against the inode's current ctime work better here?  The
inode is already in hand for the current_time() call at the top of the
function.
View on lore ↗

Last comment 2026-09-03 Re: [PATCH v2] fs/locks: filter OFD locks from /proc/locks for foreign pid namespaces Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses an issue where /proc/locks was not correctly filtering out Open File Descriptor (OFD) locks from foreign process ID namespaces. The problem arose because these locks are visible to the system but not shown in /proc/locks, leading to inconsistent results and potential confusion. The patch considers alternative approaches, including showing all locks with a negative pid for those acquired in foreign namespaces.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Tao Cui ↳ replying to Jeff Layton Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer questioned the correctness of hiding OFD locks from /proc/locks and suggested showing all locks instead, reporting foreign pid namespaces as negative values or using a similar approach to remote locks. Reviewer noted that folding the check into locks_translate_pid() would cause F_GETLK/F_OFD_GETLK to report a conflicting OFD lock with l_pid = 0 instead of -1, as documented by fcntl(2), and expressed hesitation about this change. Reviewer noted that hiding OFD locks from /proc/locks may be confusing and suggested anonymizing the owner instead, citing precedent from fdinfo and potential debugging benefits.
Show original comment 
Hi, Jeff

\u5728 2026/9/2 21:03, Jeff Layton \u5199\u9053:

---

Good point. I did consider that.  My hesitation with folding it into
locks_translate_pid() is that posix_lock_to_flock() also uses it, so
F_GETLK/F_OFD_GETLK would then report a conflicting OFD lock with
l_pid = 0 instead of the -1 that fcntl(2) documents for OFD locks.
That's why I kept the check in locks_show(), where it only affects
the /proc/locks view.

That distinction isn't introduced by this patch, though, since it
already exists for POSIX locks: since the 4.9 pid namespace filter, a
foreign-namespace POSIX lock that blocks F_GETLK is reported there
with l_pid = 0 while /proc/locks hides it.  v2 just gives OFD locks
the same treatment.

---

That's a reasonable alternative - anonymizing the owner instead of
hiding the lock does have precedent (fdinfo follows that model), and I
can see the debugging benefit of always showing locks that may block
acquisition.  I went with hiding because it preserves the /proc/locks
visibility semantics introduced in 4.9 by making OFD locks consistent
with POSIX locks.  With only the pid anonymized, the output would
still disclose which files are locked and the locked byte ranges of
tasks outside the reader's pid namespace.

If the consensus is that /proc/locks should show all locks with an
anonymized pid instead, I'm happy to rework the patch in that
direction.

Thanks,
Tao

Last comment 2026-09-03 Re: [PATCH] nfsd: switch nfsd4_open() to use vfs_lookup_open() Reviewed-by Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch updates the nfsd4_open() function in the NFS daemon to use vfs_lookup_open(), which simplifies the code and reduces complexity.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
NeilBrown ↳ replying to Jeff Layton Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
NeilBrown pointed out that lookup_open() does not require I_MUTEX_PARENT because the opened inode will not be locked, and suggested that using the same subclass for all cases was a simpler approach reviewer pointed out a lockdep issue that needs to be addressed, referencing a link to the specific patchset on sashiko.dev
Show original comment 
This is of no consequence.
start_creating() doesn't really need I_MUTEX_PARENT as the child will
never be locked.  Only start_removing() and start_renamed() need that.
But it was simpler to use the same subclass for all cases.

lookup_open() could use I_MUTEX_PARENT but doesn't need to as the opened
inode will not be locked, whether it is created or not.

---

Thanks,
NeilBrown

[PATCH v1 0/2] fuse: fixes for iomap bugs reported by Sashiko 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] fuse: don't shorten the folio descriptor at LLONG_MAX
  • [2/2] fuse: zero the correct range on a short read reply
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series fixes two iomap bugs reported by Sashiko in the FUSE (Filesystem in Userspace) kernel module. The first bug occurs when a folio descriptor is shortened at LLONG_MAX, and the second bug incorrectly zeros the wrong range on a short read reply. The patches address these issues to ensure correct behavior and prevent potential data corruption.

Last comment 2026-09-01 [PATCH v1] mm/memcontrol: drop non-hierarchical memcg-wide stats in non-v1 kernels Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes the Linux kernel's memory control group (memcg) statistics by dropping non-hierarchical memcg-wide stats in kernels that don't support version 1 of memcg. This is done by compiling out the non-hierarchical arrays when CONFIG_MEMCG_V1=n, which reduces the number of cachelines pulled in during flush operations and makes them cheaper. The patch also includes benchmark results showing a 2-4% reduction in flush time on an 80-cpu x86_64 machine with 500 cgroups. The changes do not affect kernel builds with CONFIG_MEMCG_V1=y, which continue to support the legacy memory.stat and memory.numa_stat readers.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Show original comment 
Can we query the boot parameter instead of the config option here?
IIUC this should work even if v1 is only disabled at boot.

They propose populating state_local and events_local arrays from aggregated state/events arrays in a .bind callback.
Show original comment 
This seems like a good idea to me. Maybe we should take it one step
further and gate it behind cgroup_subsys_on_dfl() to cover any
CONFIG_MEMCG_V1=y kernel that doesn't use/mount v1? Not a memcg expert
but afaict, if the kernel then switches from default (v2) to legacy
(v1), we could populate the state_local and events_local arrays from
the aggregated state / events arrays in a .bind callback since
switching from v2 to v1 is only allowed if there's no memcg subtrees.

Last comment 2026-09-02 [PATCH v2] mm/memcontrol: skip non-hierarchical memcg-wide stats on the default hierarchy Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes memory control group (memcg) statistics by skipping non-hierarchical memcg-wide stats on the default hierarchy, reducing flush costs and improving performance. The optimization is based on the observation that only legacy memory.stat and memory.numa_stat readers access these stats, which are not used on the default hierarchy. By skipping these stats, the patch reduces cacheline accesses and improves flush function performance by 2.2%. The change also includes a mechanism to handle cases where a controller is moved from the default hierarchy to the legacy v1 hierarchy at runtime.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Yosry Ahmed ↳ replying to Joanne Koong Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Yosry Ahmed questioned whether the added complexity of handling hierarchy changes is necessary for the optimization to work when switching from v2 to v1, suggesting an alternative approach using a boot parameter instead.
Show original comment 
I wonder if the complexity is warranted for this optimization to work
when switching from v2 -> v1, or if we should just query the boot
parameter.
View on lore ↗
Signals: complexity, alternative
Joanne Koong (author) ↳ replying to Yosry Ahmed Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged a race condition pointed out by Sashiko, agreed to drop the default hierarchy optimization for v3 and instead query the boot parameter.
Show original comment 
I think you're right that we should just query the boot parameter.
Sashiko pointed out a race [1] in the opposite direction between the
flush and the bind where the flush can overwrite the local[i] value
stored by the bind, which is much worse than the above case where the
bind overwrites the local[i] value stored by the flush.

I'll drop the default hierarchy stuff for v3.

Thanks,
Joanne

[PATCH v3] mm/memcontrol: skip non-hierarchical memcg-wide stats when v1 is unavailable Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the memory controller (memcg) by skipping non-hierarchical memcg-wide statistics when version 1 is not supported or has been disabled on a specific cgroup. This reduces unnecessary writes to cachelines, making flushes cheaper and improving performance. The patch achieves this by compiling out the non-hierarchical arrays if CONFIG_MEMCG_V1 is not set, and skipping updates on these arrays even if v1 is enabled but blocked on a particular controller. As a result, the time taken for mem_cgroup_css_rstat_flush() decreases by around 4-5% in benchmarking tests.

Last comment 2026-09-02 Re: [RFC PATCH v4 2/3] mm/zswap: replace the zswap_pools list with a fixed pools array Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch replaces the dynamic zswap_pools list with a fixed pools array to improve performance and reduce the likelihood of failure modes such as pool corruption or memory leaks. The new approach uses an idr (indirectly referenced) data structure to manage the pools, which allows for more efficient lookups and insertions. A find_or_create function is also introduced to simplify the process of finding or creating a zswap pool.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Jianyue Wu ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Jianyue Wu noted that hitting the 15-pool cap would be an annoying failure mode, even if it should be rare, and suggested switching to an IDR with IDs in the 1..255 range, keeping 0 as the invalid entry value, and factoring the lookup/allocation path into a zswap_pool_find_or_create() helper.
Show original comment 
Agreed. Hitting the 15-pool cap would be an annoying failure mode, even
if it should be rare.
I will switch this to an IDR with IDs in the 1..255 range, keeping 0 as
the invalid entry value, and factor the lookup/allocation path into a
zswap_pool_find_or_create() helper.

Best regards,
Jianyue

Last comment 2026-09-01 Re: [PATCH v3] mm: remove min_free_kbytes adjustment for THP Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch removes the adjustment for minimum free kbytes (min_free_kbytes) when Transparent Huge Pages (THP) are enabled. The current implementation adjusts min_free_kbytes to accommodate 512M pageblocks, but this can lead to issues such as increased fragmentation, reduced conversion rates, and poor performance on smaller machines. By removing this adjustment, the patch aims to address a deeper problem of oversized pageblock sizes, which can cause problems even when min_free_kbytes is set correctly.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Zi Yan ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Zi Yan expressed concerns that lowering pageblock size would require maintaining multiple kernel versions for different needs, and instead suggested enabling better compaction to generate THPs bigger than a pageblock size, as proposed by Rik reviewer questioned the removal of min_free_kbytes adjustment for THP, suggesting that extra free memory is still necessary and can be achieved by adjusting min_free_kbytes at machine boot time
Show original comment 
But lowering pageblock size requires a kernel compilation. That means
maintaining two sets of kernels for different needs. The ultimate
solution is to enable better compaction to generate THPs bigger than
a pageblock size, like Rik's super-pageblock proposal.

After removing automatic min_free_kbytes boosting, user can still
increase it via sysctl to restore the old free memory head room.
It is much easier, right?

---

Extra free memory is still necessary. min_free_kbytes can be adjusted
at machine boot time to achieve it, right?

Best Regards,
Yan, Zi
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Johannes Weiner (author) ↳ replying to Zi Yan Neutral LLM
Author responded to concerns about the impact of removing min_free_kbytes adjustment for THP on smaller machines, explaining that fragmentation events would be more severe and compaction would become less efficient due to oversized chunks.
Show original comment 
...or whether we can say, at that point, use gigablocks/cma+hugetlb.

And then the static pageblock size for the fallback logic etc. can be
a smaller, saner default for everybody.

Because the point you didn't address: it doesn't make really sense to
have 512M pageblocks on smaller machines, beyond the min_free_kbytes
issue: Fragmentation events will poison half a gig at once,
should_try_claim_block() becomes harder which results in less
conversions and more allocations falling through to stealing, page
isolation is more likely to fail, compaction locks and operates on
oversized chunks which is bad for latency and concurrency...

Seems to me the excessive min_free_kbytes is just a symptom of a
deeper problem.

Signals: acknowledged a deeper problem, explained reasoning
Zi Yan ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Zi Yan questioned why a large pageblock would still result in many fallbacks, suggesting it indicates a problem with unmovable pages being allocated and remaining free across multiple pageblocks, and asked for elaboration on how larger pageblocks cause bigger issues. Reviewer noted that the excessive min_free_kbytes is a symptom of an underlying issue with the anti-fragmentation mechanism, which fails to work as expected and requires high min_free_kbytes for khugepaged to function; they suggested exploring alternative solutions such as watermark boost or reclaiming extra free memory instead of reserving it via min_free_kbytes Reviewer noted that the patch's assumption of 'set and forget' min_free_kbytes recalculation does not hold true for khugepaged code, which recalculates it whenever THP/mTHP/shmem THP enablements change.
Show original comment 
I actually wonder why such a big pageblock would still result in a lot
of fallbacks. Basically it indicates at some point kernel allocates a
lot of unmovable pages that use many 512MB pageblocks and the life time
of these unmovable pages are so diverse, leading to all these pageblocks
remain unmovable and free pages spread across all these pageblocks. I
thought bigger pageblocks can keep unmovable pages constrained within
fewer pageblocks, leaving more contiguous free memory.

Do you mind elaborating more on bigger pageblocks cause bigger issues?
Maybe it is something more fundamental related to our unmovable page
management (which almost does not exit)? It seems to me that the large
pageblock amplifies the issues.

---

Yes, our anti-fragmentation mechanism does not work as we expected,
so that we need an excessive min_free_kbytes to get khugepaged working.
I wonder why reclaim cannot get the extra free memory instead of
reserving it via min_free_kbytes. Maybe we need a watermark boost
when some consecutive THP allocations are seen to achieve similar
effect of boosting min_free_kbytes?

---

Yes, but with current khugepaged code, min_free_kbytes is recalculated
whenever any of THP, mTHP, and shmem THP enablements is changed. After
the change, min_free_kbytes is basically a set and forget thing.

Signals: unclear, questioned, questioning the patch's readiness
Usama Arif ↳ replying to Zi Yan Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Usama Arif reported that on GPU hosts during AI-training checkpointing, they experienced significant latency spikes and workload timeouts due to unmovable memory and order-0 allocations falling back to MIGRATE_MOVABLE free lists, leading to contaminated movable pageblocks with unmovable pages that caused sustained compaction activity.
Show original comment 
The issue that finally pushed us toward 2 MiB pageblocks occurred on
GPU hosts during AI-training checkpointing. We observed significant
latency spikes and workload timeouts.

These hosts had substantial unmovable memory, and order-0 allocations
from TCP paths repeatedly fell back to MIGRATE_MOVABLE free lists.
With 512 MiB pageblocks, try_to_claim_block() often could not meet
the half-pageblock threshold, so allocation fell through to stealing
individual pages.

This contaminated very large movable pageblocks with unmovable pages.
Compaction then repeatedly scanned those pageblocks but could not
evacuate the unmovable allocations. We observed sustained compaction
activity during the latency spikes.
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes, concern about performance impact

But I think there are levels of argument quality:

1. This code is old as in time
2. This code is old as in the surroundings have changed
3. This code is not needed due to sha1, sha2, sha3 supplanting it thusly: ...
4. This code is not making a difference in represenative tests

The patch is at 2 and I would really prefer we get to 3 or 4. That's
not the same as saying we should stop making changes.

mechanism it references is about passive fragmentation avoidance by

Last comment 2026-09-04 Re: [RFC PATCH] mm: vmscan: avoid anon scanning for GFP_NOIO with low swapcache Neutral LLM

Patch aims to avoid anon scanning for GFP_NOIO with low swapcache, but author is open to revising the approach based on feedback.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Johannes Weiner Neutral LLM
Raised concerns about the patch's approach and asked for more information on the workload and reclaim cycle. Suggested that aging anon alongside file may have value in certain scenarios.
Signals: NEEDS_WORK
Bo Zhang ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Neutral LLM
Provided additional context on the workload and reclaim cycle, explaining that the NOIO allocations are correct but the problem lies in the reclaim side. Described a specific scenario where the patch's approach may not be optimal.
Show original comment 
On Thu, Sep 03, 2026 at 09:03:04AM -0400, Johannes Weiner wrote:
> On Thu, Sep 03, 2026 at 12:01:31PM +0800, Bo Zhang wrote:
> > We have observed some cases where memory is allocated with GFP_NOIO, so
> > we cannot reclaim any anon folios unless they are in swapcache. We can
> > end up spending more than 150 ms looping in `shrink_folio_list()` scanning
> > non-swapcache folios without reclaiming a single folio. This is pure
> > overhead.
>
> Not entirely. There is some value in aging anon alongside file, so
> that the next __GFP_IO reclaimer doesn't look at a stale list.

You're right, "pure overhead" was too strong - aging anon does have
value for a later __GFP_IO reclaimer, and I don't intend to skip it in
general. Let me describe the case in full, because the reclaim cycle
itself already provides that aging on a later pass, which is what makes
me think the trade-off here leans the other way.

> Can you describe a bit more about what you observed? What workload is
> running, maybe you have a stack trace of which NOIO requests are
> routinely getting stuck in reclaim?

The workload is app launching on Android. The NOIO allocations come from
dm-verity hash-block reads via dm-bufio, which legitimately use GFP_NOIO
because they run underneath the IO path:

  worker_thread
   process_scheduled_works
    verity_work
     verity_verify_io
      verity_hash_for_block
       verity_verify_level
        dm_bufio_read_with_ioprio
         new_read
          __bufio_new
           alloc_buffer
            gfp_mask: GFP_NOIO | __GFP_NORETRY | __GFP_NOMEMALLOC | __GFP_NOWARN

So the NOIO use itself is correct; the problem is on the reclaim side.

Here is the full picture of one such direct reclaim. It runs two rounds
of do_try_to_free_pages(); the target is 32 folios.

Round 1 - partial (shared) memcg walk, 169.20 ms, 0 folios reclaimed
--------------------------------------------------------------------
  prio 12->1 (~1.3 ms):
    cache_trim_mode is on, so get_scan_count() picks SCAN_FILE. Only the
    file side is scanned. Because this is a shared/partial walk, each
    priority only visits a handful of memcgs before the iterator is
    handed off, so very few memcgs are looked at on the way down:
    428 file folios scanned, 0 reclaimed.

  prio 0 (~167.9 ms):
    priority hits 0 without meeting the target, so get_scan_count()
    forces SCAN_EQUAL. The walk lands on a single memcg with a large,
    unswapped anon LRU and a tiny file LRU:

      inactive_anon ~335 MB, inactive_file ~4 MB   (~84:1)
      memcg swap usage ~3.6 MB, so swapcache is negligible

    shrink_lruvec() now keeps feeding that huge anon list into
    shrink_folio_list() - ~2400 shrink_folio_list() calls, ~93,000 anon
    folios scanned - and every folio hits the !__GFP_IO keep_locked path
    (not in swapcache, needs a swap slot). This single shrink_lruvec()
    pass alone is ~168 ms with 0 folios reclaimed.

  Round 1 ends with nr_reclaimed = 0 < target, so reclaim retries with
  sc->memcg_full_walk = 1.

Round 2 - full memcg walk, 2.37 ms, 68 folios reclaimed
-------------------------------------------------------
  With memcg_full_walk = 1, priority resets to 12 and every priority
  now visits the complete subtree (~78 memcgs). Progress is made
  entirely from the file side:

    prio 12: ...  0 reclaimed
    prio 11: ...  0
    prio 10: ...  3
    prio  9: ...  8
    prio  8: ... 18
    prio  7: ... 38   -> cumulative 68 >= target 32, done

  All 68 reclaimed folios come from file LRUs; anon contributes 0.

So the whole 168 ms is spent scanning an anon list that cannot yield a
single folio under GFP_NOIO, and the actual progress comes from file in
a fast full-walk round that follows.

> 150ms sounds awful indeed. Is this cumulative for a whole reclaim
> cycle or single shrink_folio_list() runs?

It is a single shrink_lruvec() invocation on that one memcg, as above -
not accumulated across the cycle. Each shrink_folio_list() only handles
SWAP_CLUSTER_MAX folios and is fast on its own; it's the prio-0 while
loop over the huge anon list that adds up to ~168 ms.

On the aging trade-off
----------------------
I take your point that this pass would otherwise have aged anon for the
next __GFP_IO reclaimer. But in this cycle that benefit is small and the
cost is large:

- The aging is not lost so much as deferred. Round 2 (and any later
  __GFP_IO reclaimer) still walks the full subtree; anon that genuinely
  needs IO to be reclaimed gets aged/reclaimed then, once IO is allowed.
- The 168 ms is spent scanning ~93k anon folios that, by construction of
  GFP_NOIO + negligible swapcache, cannot be reclaimed on this pass at
  all - the aging is the only product, and it comes at the price of a
  ~168 ms stall in a latency-sensitive path.

That's why I'd argue the balance tips towards skipping anon here rather
than aging it. To keep the change narrow, the check only triggers at
priority 0 (where SCAN_EQUAL is forced) and only when swapcache is far
below the anon LRU (below min(anon >> 6, SWAP_CLUSTER_MAX)), i.e. when
essentially no anon on the list is reclaimable without IO. Outside that
corner anon is scanned and aged exactly as before.

And skipping anon in this corner doesn't cost us the aging in practice:
with no reclaimable anon left to scan, Round 1 simply finishes quickly
and reclaim proceeds to the full-walk retry (Round 2), which resets to
priority 12 and walks the whole subtree - anon included - so anon still
gets aged there, just without the ~168 ms detour first.

Thanks,
Bo

[PATCH v5 0/7] move stock from mem_cgroup to page_counter 7 patches
Show 7 individual patches
  • [1/7] mm/memcontrol: flatten try_charge_memcg control flow
  • [2/7] mm/page_counter: report the number of pages charged
  • [3/7] mm/page_counter: introduce per-page_counter stock
  • [4/7] mm/page_counter: use stock in page_counter_try_charge
  • [5/7] mm/page_counter: introduce an asynchronous drainer
  • [6/7] mm/memcontrol: convert memcg to use page_counter_stock
  • [7/7] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page_counter
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series moves the stock management from mem_cgroup to page_counter, allowing each counter to own and drain its own cache. This eliminates the 7 memcg-per-cpu slot limit, random cross-memcg stock drains, and slot traversal. The stock is now a property of the counter rather than the cpu, making it reachable remotely and eliminating the need for synchronous draining. The patch also introduces independent stock management for additional page_counters in each memcg, enabling features like tiered memory limits. The approach taken preserves as much of the old semantics as possible while addressing existing issues with stock management.

Last comment 2026-09-02 [PATCH v2] selftests/cgroup: account for zswap shrinker writeback Neutral LLM

The patch modifies the selftests/cgroup test_zswap.c to account for zswap shrinker writeback when checking for invasive cgroup shrink behavior. It introduces a new function zswap_shrinker_enabled() and updates the test_no_invasive_cgroup_shrink() function to compare writeback counts before and after the aggressor runs, ensuring that the victim cgroup does not perform additional writebacks.

Show original comment 
On Tue, Sep 1, 2026 at 12:13 PM Joshua Hahn <joshua.hahnjy@gmail.com> wrote:
>
> The test_no_invasive_cgroup_shrink selftest checks that when a cgroup
> has zswapped out more pages than memory.zswap.max, that it does not

I think writeback in the control group (zw_group) happens right during
the first round of memory allocation, but there shouldn't be any on
the second round (as the second round involves writing to wb_group
only). So if we read zswap writeback counter of that cgroup (zw_group)
right after that first allocation round, and make sure delta = 0 after
the second round, then that should work for both shrinker_enabled = Y
v.s N.

If it's still too noisy, then I'm fine with skipping it when
shrinker_enabled = Y.

Last comment 2026-09-04 [PATCH 00/12] mm/collapse: separate a collapse from its callers Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch set separates the collapse engine from its callers, introducing a clear interface and policy between them. The collapse engine is moved to khugepaged.c, but it now has an interface that allows callers to specify their own policies and interact with the engine without reaching into each other's code. The patch also moves MADV_COLLAPSE to madvise.c, where it belongs, and introduces four new calls: collapse_control_init, collapse_policy_set, collapse_scan_pmd, and collapse_run_pmd. This separation improves modularity and maintainability of the code.

14 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing a concern about the mmgrab() reference in madvise_collapse(), acknowledging it's redundant and every caller already holds an mm_users reference. The author promises to drop the mmgrab()/mmdrop() pair.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

madvise_collapse() holds an mmgrab() reference across its work. It is
redundant. Every caller already holds mm_users:

 - madvise(2) works on current->mm, which lives as long as the task is in
   the syscall;
 - process_madvise(2) reaches a remote mm through mm_access(), which takes
   an mm_users reference and holds it until the syscall returns;
 - io_uring passes current->mm;
 - DAMON takes one with get_task_mm() and drops it after the call.

Drop the mmgrab()/mmdrop() pair.

Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the naming of the bitmap in khugepaged.c, explaining that it represents PTEs accepted as collapse sources and not just their presence. They agreed to rename it and update comments accordingly without any functional change.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

The name says less than the bit means.  A set bit means not only that the
PTE is present, but also that it passed the other checks: uffd,
lazyfree, anonymity, sharing.  The PTE can be considered a collapse
source.

mthp_collapse() then reads the bitmap starting at the PMD order, so the
bitmap is not specific to mTHP either.

Name it for what a set bit means, and update the comments that named it.

No functional change.

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, no clear resolution signal
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Neutral LLM
Author acknowledged that the users of collapse and its machinery are intertwined in khugepaged.c, agreed to separate them by adding a new header file mm/collapse.h for shared definitions, and confirmed that no functional change is introduced.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

khugepaged.c holds both the users of collapse and the machinery that
performs it.  The daemon's scan loop, the sysfs tunables, MADV_COLLAPSE's
entry point and the collapse itself all sit in one file and reach into
each other freely.  Nothing marks where a user ends and the engine
begins.

Start drawing that line.

Add mm/collapse.h for what the two sides have to agree on:

  - enum scan_result - what the engine hands back;
  - struct collapse_control - the state a request carries.

And two constants move with them:

  - KHUGEPAGED_MAX_PTES_LIMIT -> COLLAPSE_MAX_PTES_LIMIT;
  - KHUGEPAGED_MIN_MTHP_ORDER -> COLLAPSE_MIN_MTHP_ORDER.

Neither is a fact about the daemon, so both lose the KHUGEPAGED_ prefix.

No functional change.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged, no functional change
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern that tests scattered through the collapse path decide what a collapse is allowed to do by asking whether khugepaged started it, and explained that this should be handled by having the caller fill in a struct collapse_policy with their policy before calling the collapse code. The author confirmed that khugepaged fills the policy once per scan pass, MADV_COLLAPSE once per call, and made other minor changes to handle this new structure.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

Tests scattered through the collapse path decide what a collapse is
allowed to do by asking whether khugepaged started it.  Between them they
settle:

 - which VMAs are eligible, and how hard to try for a folio;
 - how many empty, swapped-out or shared PTEs a window may contain, and
   whether a sub-PMD window is held to a stricter rule than a PMD;
 - whether a range has to look used, and whether a MADV_FREE'd page is
   left alone;
 - whether the PMD is mapped as part of the request, and whether dirty
   pages are worth writing back and retrying.

None of those is a fact about khugepaged.  Each is something the caller
decided before asking, and the collapse code should not have to look up
who called to find out.

Add struct collapse_policy for the caller to fill: khugepaged from its
own settings, MADV_COLLAPSE from the fact that a user asked explicitly.
Every test becomes a read of a field, and cc->is_khugepaged goes, having
no reader left.

khugepaged fills the policy once per scan pass, MADV_COLLAPSE once per
call.  That is the one change in behaviour.  The max_ptes_* limits and the
defrag setting behind the allocation mask are sampled once per pass rather
than on every table.  A table scanned early in a pass and one scanned late
are then judged alike.

collapse_file() also drops a NULL check on the collapse_control.  It has
one call site, reached only from collapse_single_pmd(), which dereferences
cc unconditionally, so the check was already dead.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

diff --git a/mm/collapse.h b/mm/collapse.h
index 1c40229b9554..05282eed9a35 100644

@@ -48,8 +48,46 @@ enum scan_result {
 	SCAN_PAGE_DIRTY_OR_WRITEBACK,
 };
 
+/* What a collapse is allowed to do, decided by the caller that asks for it */
+struct collapse_policy {
+	/* Limits, stated per PMD; HPAGE_PMD_NR means "no limit" */
+	unsigned int max_ptes_none;
+	unsigned int max_ptes_swap;
+	unsigned int max_ptes_shared;
+
+	/*
+	 * Hold a sub-PMD window to a stricter rule than a PMD: no swapped-out
+	 * and no shared PTEs at all, and max_ptes_none as
+	 * collapse_max_ptes_none() scales it.
+	 */
+	bool strict_sub_pmd;
+
+	/*
+	 * Collapse only where it looks worth doing: require some sign the
+	 * range is in use, and leave clean lazyfree folios for reclaim rather
+	 * than collapsing them into a folio that is not lazyfree.
+	 */
+	bool skip_lazyfree;
+	bool require_referenced;
+
+	/*
+	 * Finish the job rather than leaving it half done for a fault to pick
+	 * up: map the PMD over a file collapse before returning, and write
+	 * dirty pages back and retry once instead of refusing them.  Both cost
+	 * latency the caller has to be willing to pay.
+	 */
+	bool install_pmd;
+	bool writeback_dirty;
+
+	/* How hard to try for a destination folio */
+	gfp_t gfp;
+
+	/* Which VMAs are eligible, as thp_vma_allowable_orders() spells it */
+	enum tva_type tva_type;
+};
+
 struct collapse_control {
-	bool is_khugepaged;
+	struct collapse_policy policy;
 
 	/* Num pages scanned per node */
 	u32 node_load[MAX_NUMNODES];

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a concern, explained their reasoning
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Positive LLM
The author is addressing a concern about the naming of collapse_control_init_scan(), which was resetting values for each scan instead of once per control. The author agrees that the name points at the wrong thing and suggests renaming it to collapse_scan_reset() to reflect its actual purpose, with no functional change.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

collapse_control_init_scan() resets what one scan accumulates: the node
load, the allocation nodemask and the eligible-PTE bitmap.  It runs before
every PTE table a scan is given, not once per control, so its name points
at the wrong thing.

Call it collapse_scan_reset().

Preparation for giving a control a real init and release, run once each
for a whole series of scans.  Two names a word apart would then stand for
two different jobs.

No functional change.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: agreement, clarification
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed concerns about the boundary between scanning and collapsing in collapse_single_pmd(), agreeing to split it into two separate functions: collapse_scan_pmd() for scanning, which reads under mmap_lock, and collapse_run_pmd() for collapsing, which wants the lock given up first. The author also explained changes to tracing and how scan results are communicated via the collapse_control structure.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

A collapse is two jobs.  One reads a PTE table under mmap_lock and decides
whether the range is worth collapsing.  The other allocates, isolates,
copies and flushes, and wants the lock given up first.

collapse_single_pmd() did both, so the boundary between them was somewhere
in the middle of a function.

Give each half its own function:

  - collapse_scan_pmd() scans one table and only reads.  The anonymous
    scan that used to carry that name keeps its body as
    collapse_scan_anon_pmd(), and collapse_scan_pmd() is now the entry
    that picks the anonymous or the file side.

  - collapse_run_pmd() does the collapse the scan asked for.
    SCAN_SUCCEED from the scan means there is something to run; anything
    else is why there is not.

collapse_single_pmd() is now the two of them with the mmap_lock drop in
between, so its callers see what they saw before.

Scan results (beyond SCAN_SUCCEED) communicated via collapse_control
structure: the orders, the referenced and swapped-out counts, and for a
file the file itself and the offset in it.

A file collapse works on the page cache and never sees a VMA.  The scan
takes the file reference while it still has VMA and the run unpins it
when it is done.

Tracing changes with it.  mm_khugepaged_scan_pmd now fires before
mm_collapse_huge_page instead of after it.  Its status field already reads
SCAN_SUCCEED for an accepted table, so what the collapse then made of that
table is mm_collapse_huge_page's to report, per order.

The two calls to that tracepoint become one.  They differed in what the
collapse between them changed; with the collapse no longer here, both
carry the same arguments.  failed_pfn is set only where a PTE was refused,
so it is -1 exactly when the result is SCAN_SUCCEED.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged concerns, explained reasoning
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed concerns about the locking contract in collapse_scan_pmd() and collapse_run_pmd(), agreeing to open-code the entry point in the two callers, khugepaged's walk and madvise_collapse(). Each scans under its own lock and drops it before running the collapse. The lock is given up and taken again at the same points as before, with no functional change.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

collapse_scan_pmd() and collapse_run_pmd() each have a clear locking
contract.  The scan is called with mmap_lock held for reading and returns
with it still held.  The collapse is called without it.

collapse_single_pmd() kept that boundary inside itself.  It dropped the
lock on some paths and not others, and reported which by way of a bool its
callers had to carry along and then act on.

Open-code it in the two callers.  Each scans under the lock it already
holds and, on SCAN_SUCCEED, gives the lock up before running the collapse.
khugepaged's lock_dropped and madvise_collapse()'s mmap_unlocked both go:
the code dropping the lock is now the code that wanted to know.

khugepaged's walk carries on to the next table while the scan keeps
refusing, and ends once a collapse has taken the lock from under it.
madvise_collapse() re-finds its VMA after a collapse, which it did before,
and now uses a NULL vma to say that it has to.  It still reports the drop
to its own caller, from the line that does it.

The lock is given up and taken again at the same points as before.  No
functional change.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

[PATCH 05/12] mm/collapse: state what a collapse may do in the policy Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch introduces a new struct collapse_policy that allows the caller to specify what a collapse is allowed to do, such as setting limits on PTEs and determining whether to hold sub-PMD windows to stricter rules. The policy is filled in by khugepaged once per scan pass and by MADV_COLLAPSE once per call. This change simplifies the code by removing the need for tests scattered throughout the collapse path to determine what a collapse can do, instead allowing them to simply read from the policy struct.

[PATCH 07/12] mm/collapse: name the per-table scan reset for what it resets Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch renames the function collapse_control_init_scan() to collapse_scan_reset(), which more accurately reflects its purpose of resetting the node load, allocation nodemask, and eligible-PTE bitmap for each scan. This change is a preparation step towards giving controls their own init and release functions, which will be run once per control rather than before every scan.

[PATCH 08/12] mm/collapse: separate scanning a PTE table from collapsing it Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch separates the scanning of a PTE table from collapsing it into two distinct functions: collapse_scan_pmd() and collapse_run_pmd(). The scan function reads the PTE table under mmap_lock and decides whether to collapse, while the run function performs the actual collapse. This change improves code organization and allows for better tracing and communication between the scan and run phases.

[PATCH 09/12] mm/collapse: open-code collapse_single_pmd() in its two callers Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch removes the function collapse_single_pmd() and instead open-codes its logic in two callers, khugepaged's walk and madvise_collapse(). The original function had a complex locking contract that was simplified by giving up the lock before running the collapse. This change does not introduce any functional changes but simplifies the code.

[PATCH 10/12] mm/collapse: work out the orders a VMA allows once per VMA Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the calculation of allowed page orders in a VMA by computing it once per VMA instead of once per PTE table. This is done to reduce redundant work and improve performance. The patch also introduces a new parameter, `enabled_orders`, which is passed from the caller to the function that computes the allowed orders. This change allows for more efficient handling of hugepage-enabled knobs written during a walk.

[PATCH 11/12] mm/collapse: declare the collapse interface in collapse.h Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch declares the collapse interface in collapse.h, which includes functions and variables related to memory collapsing. The interface consists of four main calls: collapse_control_init, collapse_scan_pmd, collapse_run_pmd, and collapse_control_release. These functions are used for collapsing large pages and are currently static in khugepaged.c. The patch also renames hugepage_vma_revalidate to collapse_vma_revalidate as it is now part of the collapse interface. This change prepares the ground for implementing MADV_COLLAPSE in madvise.c, but does not introduce any functional changes.

[PATCH 12/12] mm/collapse: implement MADV_COLLAPSE in madvise.c Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch moves the implementation of MADV_COLLAPSE from khugepaged.c to madvise.c, where it belongs among other madvise operations. The move simplifies the code and removes unnecessary dependencies between the daemon's file and syscall code. The patch also includes a stub for !CONFIG_TRANSPARENT_HUGEPAGE configurations. The MADV_COLLAPSE operation remains a caller of the interface used by khugepaged, with no changes to its functionality.

Last comment 2026-09-03 [RFC PATCH 0/5] mm: sub-folio dirty tracking for PTE-mapped mmap writes 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm: let folio_mkclean() report which pages had dirty PTEs
  • [2/5] mm: add a_ops->dirty_folio_range() and use the mkclean dirty harvest
  • [3/5] mm: keep the mmap dirty range down to the faulting page
  • [4/5] iomap: narrow page_mkwrite() dirtying to the faulting page
  • [5/5] xfs: track mmap dirty state per block
Neutral LLM

This RFC patch series proposes a new approach to tracking dirty state for PTE-mapped mmap writes, which currently dirties the entire folio even if only a subset of pages is written. The proposed solution harvests the hardware's record of which parts of the folio were written through a mapping and hands the filesystem the runs that were dirty. This is achieved by modifying the iomap_writeback_folio() function to submit only the dirty ranges and adding a new a_ops->dirty_folio_range() interface.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Pedro Falcato Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about the complexity of the proposed solution and suggested a single method of dirtying folios. Also questioned whether sub-page accounting makes sense, given that the whole folio is still dirty for reclaim purposes.
Show original comment 
On Thu, Sep 03, 2026 at 07:29:38PM +0100, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
> 
> A store through a shared file mapping dirties the whole folio. With large
> page cache folios that turns a 4K store into 2M of writeback: one dirty
> bit per folio, and writeback has no way to know which part changed.
> 
> XFS already knows better. iomap tracks dirty state per block and
> iomap_writeback_folio() submits only the dirty ranges, and the buffered
> write path sets just the range it copied. Only the mmap path throws that
> away, because iomap_dirty_folio() covers the whole folio.
> 

Hmm, I believe Willy already had some patches; I don't know what came of
them (I think he's out for now).

> Narrowing the dirtying at page_mkwrite() time does not work on its own:
> set_pte_range() batch-maps a whole folio writable on the first shared
> write fault, so the stores that follow never fault and never reach the
> filesystem.

Help me out here: in which case does this happen? page fault handling is a
mess... I think page_mkwrite is always called, no? in do_shared_fault().

> 
> So harvest the hardware instead. folio_clear_dirty_for_io() already calls
> folio_mkclean(), whose rmap walk reads pte_dirty() for every entry of the
> folio and throws it away. Those bits are the only record of which parts
> of a large folio were written through a mapping. Collect them there and
> hand the filesystem the runs that were dirty, through a new
> a_ops->dirty_folio_range().

I think this goes against what we're trying to pull off. We want a single
method of dirtying folios, not 3.

Ideally(tm) we would have a single mkwrite interface.

> 
> All of this is about PTE-mapped folios. A PMD-mapped folio has a single
> dirty bit for the 2M it maps, so there is nothing finer to harvest, and
> it keeps writing back whole. Keeping shared write faults off PMDs is a
> separate patch and not part of this posting.
> 
> On a 512M file in 2M folios on XFS, storing one byte per folio and
> calling msync() wrote 512M before and writes 1M after, with identical
> minor fault counts.

But the effects are definitely nice though :)

> 
> Not addressed here:
> 
>  - Dirty accounting stays folio-granular. A 4K store still counts as 2M
>    against dirty_ratio and balance_dirty_pages().

That is correct; I don't think doing subpage accounting makes any sense,
the whole folio is still dirty, and for reclaim purposes can't be thrown
away (unless you added gnarly split logic for file folios as well).

>  - iomap_page_mkwrite() still allocates blocks for the whole folio.
>  - Filesystems without per-block dirty state see no change.
> 
> Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) (5):
>   mm: let folio_mkclean() report which pages had dirty PTEs
>   mm: add a_ops->dirty_folio_range() and use the mkclean dirty harvest
>   mm: keep the mmap dirty range down to the faulting page
>   iomap: narrow page_mkwrite() dirtying to the faulting page
>   xfs: track mmap dirty state per block
>

> > > +/**
> > > + * arm_smmu_queue_max_n_shift() - pick the log2 depth of a queue
> > > + * @hw_shift: log2 depth the hardware allows, capped for natural alignment
> > > + * @ent_sz_shift: log2 of the queue entry size in bytes
> > > + * @want: number of entries asked for, or zero to use @hw_shift
> > > + *
> > > + * @want is rounded down to a power of two. It never sizes a queue below one
> > > + * page, because coherent DMA is page granular: a shallower queue occupies the
> > > + * same memory as one that fills the page, and arm_smmu_init_one_queue() stops
> > > + * shrinking at a page too.
> > 
> > Well, since we want the cmdq depth, why not name the parameter
> > "max_cmdq_depth"? Then, no rounding and more straightforward.
> 
> The max_ prefix is better, thanks. cmdq_entries does not say it is a
> ceiling.
> 
> On the units, the page floor has to stay whatever we call the parameter.
> 
> Ask for 8 entries and max_n_shift comes out as 3, which the
> CMDQ_BATCH_ENTRIES check in arm_smmu_device_hw_probe() rejects with
> -ENXIO. The floor gives you 256 entries on a 4K kernel instead of a failed
> probe.
> 
> So log2 removes the rounding but not the flooring, and the number asked for
> still is not always the number you get. I would rather keep entries than
> make people convert.
> 
> The rounding is not silent, either: arm_smmu_init_one_queue() already
> prints "allocated %u entries for cmdq", so the effective depth lands in
> dmesg whichever unit the parameter takes.
> 
> On the name itself, "depth" reads to me as a number of entries rather than
> its log2, so max_cmdq_depth=8 meaning 256 seems more surprising than the
> rounding it replaces.

Well, maybe "cmdq_max_n_shift" is a better one over "depth".

Given CMDQ_BATCH_ENTRIES is used, I think cmdq_max_entries works.

Nicolin
Signals: POSITIVE
Re: [PATCH v4 1/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Add a cmdq_entries module parameter Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Re: [PATCH 07/25] mm/fbatch: LRU_NEXT_ACTIVATE bit to optimize folio_activate() Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-09-03 Re: [PATCH 07/25] mm/fbatch: LRU_NEXT_ACTIVATE bit to optimize folio_activate() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch introduces a new bit, LRU_NEXT_ACTIVATE, to optimize the folio_activate() function in the mm/fbatch module. The goal is to reduce the number of cache line flushes and improve performance on certain architectures. To achieve this, the patch defines a set_bit_release() function that uses atomic operations with release semantics, which ensures that the bit is set before the operation is visible to other CPUs. This approach provides better results than alternative methods such as using smp_mb__ + set_bit() or test_and_set_bit(). The patch also includes architecture-specific implementations for x86, arm64, ppc, and riscv, where necessary.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Hugh Dickins ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the additional lru_next transitions in the patch are unprotected by the lruvec lock and suggested adding an smp_mb__before_atomic() after LIST_POISON1 in lru_add_del_folio() to provide necessary protection.
Show original comment 
Thanks a lot for taking this further, and we may indeed want it later;
but you guess correctly that I don't want to go there for the initial
series, and I've not yet convinced myself that it's what we want anyway.

Assuming that the existing pre-series code is correct just to use
folio_set_lru() throughout, what I've added are these additional lru_next
transitions, unprotected by lruvec lock, and it's just these which need
the additional protection.

So, I haven't finished thinking on it, but what I'm currently inclined
to, is putting an smp_mb__before_atomic() after the LIST_POISON1 in
lru_add_del_folio(). That's a long way from the folio_set_lru() it's
intended for, which is unusual, but it seems more to the point than
always using folio_set_lru_release(). We can switch to a proper
folio_set_lru_release() later, if that's usually seen to work better.

Hugh

Sarthak Sharma ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Positive LLM
Acknowledged Kiryl's suggestion and offered to respin the patch with the changes.
Show original comment 
Hi Kiryl!

On 9/1/26 4:05 PM, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> On Tue, Sep 01, 2026 at 02:04:51PM +0530, Sarthak Sharma wrote:
>> __gup_test_ioctl() calculates the end of a GUP batch using:
>>
>> next = addr + nr * PAGE_SIZE;
>>
>> If nr is too large, it can cause the next to overflow and wrap around.
>> If it wraps, the next > end check is bypassed and a large value
>> of nr is passed to the gup call, even though the pages array was
>> allocated according to gup->size. This can lead to out of bounds writes.
>>
>> Compare nr with the number of pages remaining before performing
>> the multiplication. Clamp it to remaining range so that next does
>> not overflow or exceed end.
>>
>> Fixes: 64c349f4ae78 ("mm: add infrastructure for get_user_pages_fast() benchmarking")
>> Signed-off-by: Sarthak Sharma <sarthak.sharma@arm.com>
>> ---
>>  mm/gup_test.c | 5 +++--
>>  1 file changed, 3 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)
>>
>> diff --git a/mm/gup_test.c b/mm/gup_test.c
>> index 44c1cdfb9c37..910cbef709b4 100644
>> --- a/mm/gup_test.c
>> +++ b/mm/gup_test.c
>> @@ -139,10 +139,11 @@ static int __gup_test_ioctl(unsigned int cmd,
>>  		if (nr != gup->nr_pages_per_call)
>>  			break;
>>  
>> -		next = addr + nr * PAGE_SIZE;
>> -		if (next > end) {
>> +		if (nr > (end - addr) / PAGE_SIZE) {
>>  			next = end;
>>  			nr = (next - addr) / PAGE_SIZE;
>> +		} else {
>> +			next = addr + nr * PAGE_SIZE;
>>  		}
>>  
>>  		switch (cmd) {
> 
> What about this:
> 
>               nr = min(nr, (end - addr) / PAGE_SIZE);
>               next = addr + nr * PAGE_SIZE;
> 
> Seems to be easier to follow, no?

Yup, this would be cleaner. Do you want me to respin with this changed?

Signals: requested evidence, skeptical tone
Jason Gunthorpe ↳ replying to Lorenzo (ARM) Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Jason Gunthorpe noted that the current implementation of call_rcu() on each PMD can be costly, especially for large THPs, and suggested leveraging mmu_gather to thread pages to be freed onto a linked list in struct page memory. This approach would allow for a single call_rcu using the rcu head of the struct page of the head of the list, which clears the entire linked list of pages.
Show original comment 
The less costly version is to thread the page to be freed onto the
mmu_gather through a linked list in the struct page memory. This is
super cheap since it is just a singly linked list operation.

Then when the mmu_gather is flushed it does a single call_rcu using
the rcu head of the struct page of the head of the list. The callback
clears the entire linked list of pages.

Since you have to tlb flush anyhow, it makes sense to always use the
mmu_gather. For example the design I ended up with for iommupt
accumulates all the invalidations and all the free-able memory into a
gather then invalidates and frees.

This allows maximizing the tlbi efficiency too. You can't do call_srcu
until you flush the tlb and if you call once per table then you are
also tlb flushing once per table too.

So if the kernel really does want to clear out 512 leaf tables the
optimal implementation is one range tlbi for 512 entries followed by
one call_rcu to free the memory. Hence the gather..

Jason

Lorenzo (ARM) ↳ replying to Jason Gunthorpe Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Reviewer Lorenzo (ARM) pointed out that the patch is batching up page table frees individually using call_rcu(), which he believes will not cause observable problems, and suggested that adding additional complexity to address potential issues is unnecessary without clear demonstration of their impact.
Show original comment 
I think there's some confusion here.

This isn't the path in which a page table is being freed, the _deposited_
table is zapped, in zap_deposited_table().

That is, the page table kept in reserve for THP split, that is not
currently mapped.

It amounts to a __free_pages() call.

The TLB operations are in e.g. zap_huge_pmd() etc. and nobody has
complained about inefficiencies there.

So, unless I'm missing something here, TLB flushes play no role in this
whatsoever.

The issue Kiryl raised was that instead of immediately freeing page tables,
they are now batched up individually by call_rcu().

I personally find it difficult to imagine the numbers here would be
problematic or certainly cause anything observable beyond what is
observable now.

So I'm going to have to say, unless it can be clearly demonstrated this is
problematic, I don't think there's any reason to add additional complexity
here.

Reviewer suggested packing page table pointers into a contiguous data structure to avoid pointer chasing through a linked list, potentially improving performance and reducing overhead. Reviewer Liam Howlett expressed skepticism about the need to measure munmap() overhead, suggesting it would not prove anything significant and that other system activities would dominate performance at high RCU linked list sizes.
Show original comment 
If you are going through the trouble of doing this on your side with a
custom rcu callback, it might be better to pack the pointers in a way

---

I'm not sure what that would prove besides checking if your number is
correct?  You'll see a slow down once you hit 10K of objects in the rcu
linked list.  And that will depend on what the system is doing as well,
so there will be a slow down since other things are happening on the
system before 20GB of THP is unleashed towards munmap().

One of these does use 5.12% of the RCU items that need to wait for a
grace period to be released, but testing it would be rather tricky and
the numbers won't really show much..?

Form my understanding and experience with rcu and freeing massive
numbers of objects, you gain more from the lower lock contention than
you lose when you hit 10K objects.

The trade off is in the Android setup, where they have restricted RCU to
a slower core.  Combined with another RCU setting, it was causing slow
down when we overwhelmed the queue multiple times.

So this should actually be faster unless you are doing 20GB of THP
unmapping on Android or a device configured the same way.  Considering
the cost of RAM, we should be okay for a few phone iterations.

Do you have munmap() performance dependent workload that you are
concerned about regressing?

Liam
